=== FILE: 90⧸90 Hip Rotation： Improve Hip Mobility & Spinal Flexibility [vILRq2x2lp4].en.txt ===

Reverse frog hypers. This exercise targets the glutius maximus and erector spina. Lie face down on a bench. Chest and lower abdomen on the bench and legs off the bench. Bring the feet together and abduct the knees froglike. Then raise the legs. Extend the hip and lower them back down. The glutius maximus extends the hip joint. The leg position creates an external rotation of the hip which recruits the glutius maximus muscle more. The main stabilizers are the spinal erectors and latisimus dorsy. The lumbar erectors may also contract a little dynamically depending on the pelvic movement. The more the pelvis moves between a anterior pelvic tilt and ppt posterior pelvic tilt, the more active they will be. The elbow flexors stabilize the elbow. Common mistake, overextension of the lumbar spine while raising the legs. Usually because of uncontrolled momentum. To prevent the extension, instruct the trainee to slightly contract the abdominal muscles while lifting the legs.

=== FILE: Abdominal Bracing [n0Inw5ooUmU].en.txt ===

abdominal bracing what stabilizes our spine the entire abdominal area is like a big balloon that contains all the internal organs and the spine for the balloon to maintain internal pressure and not explode or collapse we must wrap it in a tight girdle and protect it from all sides this is how our abdominal girdle is built the walls around it are actually the muscles that stabilize the abdomen and spine bracing is a technique for stabilizing the spine basically embracing we tighten all the muscles surrounding the abdomen as we contract the abdominal muscles the erector spinae muscles immediately go into joint action and support the back from the rear in essence we create a natural girdle that stabilizes and protects the spine we also contract the pelvic floor muscles from below when we want to maintain maximal posture we will inhale which causes the diaphragm to descend and increase the rigidity of the torso in all the exercises presented here the aim is to maintain the spine as in a neutral standing position to do this we will contract all the abdominal muscles without leaving the neutral position you practicing bracing in pairs the trainee stands steady with arms straight out in front his aim is to keep his arms in the same place the instructor will push his arms or legs to the sides adding the element of surprise as well in the video you can see the speedy reaction of the abdominal girdle and how all the muscles work together to maintain stability

left leg lift up slowly the right leg and stand stable on one foot the right hand grasps the right ankle with the knee flexed to the rear the left hand is thrust forward at shoulder level or upward the grasp leg reaching backward against the hand holding it the flexed hip extends to the rear with gradual adduction of the foot from the pelvis to a position in which the thigh and the arm are lunge on a chair the forward leg is placed on the chair and gradually we transfer the weight forward tilting the pelvis posteriorly to make the hip flexors more flexible the fourth exercise is lying on the back we pull the knee toward the chest and tilt the pelvis posteriorly the muscles that are stretched are the hip flexors of the straight leg one of the common problems is sensitivity when lower back is in hyperlordosis first solution would be performing the movement without the forward tilt enter the position by standing straight in the mountain pose tadasana with legs parallel at pelvic width transfer your weight to the left leg lift up slowly the right leg and stand stable on one foot the right hand grasps the right ankle with the knee flexed to the rear the left hand is upward Direction in this variation the upper part of the body remains in place the movement is now coming only from thrusting the hand and extension of the leg additionally to prevent hyperlordosis in the lower back tilt the pelvis posteriorly holding the abdominal muscles and gently contract the pelvic floor muscles another common problem is shortened hamstrings in the supporting leg one solution is is to reduce the angle of flexion at the Torso and at the hip joint second solution is to slightly bend the knee of the supporting leg third solution is to perform the pose without tilting the === FILE: Anatomical Terminology - Directional Terms [vB0Kvo-bEhI].en.txt ===

anatomical terminology directional terms anterior toward or on the front of the body in front of posterior towards or on the back of the body behind superior toward the head or upper part of a structure above inferior toward the lower part of a structure below medial toward or at the midline of the body inner side the pectoralis major is medial to the deltoid lateral away from the midline of the body outer side the abductors are on the lateral aspect of the leg proximal closer to the origin of a point of reference the elbow is proximal to the wrist distal further from the origin or point of reference download our app for fast and easy learning becoming a real professional is a couple clicks away

=== FILE: Assessing Lower Limb Functionality with the Forward Step Down Test [24N7qBmKz6s].en.txt ===

forward step down FSD the forward step down FSD is a functional test requiring a stair descent which involves weightbearing stress at various knee flexion angles as well as Dynamic muscular control the FSD test can be performed either as a screening tool or as an exercise for knee Rehabilitation studies show that moderate to poor movement quality subjects have significantly less hip abductor strength and quadriceps and ITB tfl flexibility than those with good movement quality stand on an 8 in 20 cm step the tested leg's foot is close to the step's edge and the non-tested leg is positioned in front of the step with a straight knee and an ankle in maximum dors of flexion keep your trunk straight hands on your waist and bend the knee on the tested side until the heel of the non-tested leg touches the floor do not apply any wait on the heel of the non-tested leg once it reaches the floor and immediately re-extend the knee to return to the starting position perform five consecutive FSD movements before performing the test put a sticker on the tibial tuberosity of the tested leg and a sticker on the vertical front edge of the step just under the second toe of the tested leg next next adjust the height of the step so that your participant achieves a 60% knee flexion during the test if the heel of the non-tested leg doesn't contact the floor when the other knee is bent at 60% Place wood blocks on the step to ensure that the knee reaches a 60% flexion when the heel of the non-tested leg touches the floor there are five criteria for scoring the test one arm strategy if the participant removes their arm from the waist they get one point two trunk movement if the subject leans the trunk to either side which is interpreted as recovering balance they get one point three pelvic plane if one side of the pelvis rotates in the transverse plane or elevated in the frontal plane compared with the other side one point four knee position if the knee of the tested limb moves medely in the frontal plane over the second toe of the tested foot one point if the knee moves mes medially over the medial border of the tested foot they get two points maintenance of a steady unilateral stance if the subject has to support the body weight of the non-tested limb or the foot of the tested limb moves during testing again one point a total score of zero or one is classified as good movement quality a total score of two to three is classified as moderate movement quality and a total score of four or more is classified as poor movement quality studies show that subjects with moderate to poor movement quality have significantly less hip abductor strength and quadriceps and ITB tfl flexibility compared to those with good movement quality

=== FILE: Axillary Nerve： Anatomy & Function [KH5FSDhkB7k].txt ===

 axillary nerve. The axillary nerve originates from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus, with root contributions from C5 and C6. It courses anterior to the subscapularis muscle and enters the quadrangular space. From there, it divides into two branches. The anterior branch winds around the surgical neck of the humerus to innervate the deltoid, while the posterior branch supplies the posterior deltoid and the teres minor. It also gives rise to the superior lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm, which provides sensory innervation to the skin over the inferior lateral shoulder. In summary, the axillary nerve supplies motor innervation to the deltoid and teres minor muscles, and sensory innervation to the skin over the lower deltoid and upper lateral arm. The axillary nerve is the upper deltoid and the upper lateral arm is the upper deltoid. The axillary nerve is the upper deltoid and the upper lateral arm is the upper deltoid. The axillary nerve is the upper deltoid and the upper lateral arm is the upper deltoid. The axillary nerve is the upper deltoid and the upper lateral arm is the upper deltoid.

=== FILE: Body Proportions & Squatting： How Limb Length Affects Your Squat [8XFkmB44XRY].en.txt ===

Body proportions and squatting. Body proportions can significantly impact the biomechanics and efficiency of performing a squat. Key body parts involved in this include the tibia, shinbone, the femur, thigh bone, and the torso. Individuals with longer femurss relative to their tibia and torso often find squatting more challenging. They may need to lean their torso forward more to maintain balance. For example, these two exercisers have the same length of tibia and torso, but one has a longer thigh. Notice the difference in the angle created between the thigh and the torso. In this case, any attempt to lift the torso will cause the trainee to fall backward. On the left, we can see an exerciser with a proportionally long femur compared to a short tibia and short torso. Those with proportionally shorter femurss or long tibia tend to keep their torso more upright during squatting. A short femur is less than 25% of the total height. This trainee has a relatively long tibia compared to the femur, so she can keep her torso upright. Torso length and squatting. The length of the torso in relation to the length of the legs is also an essential parameter in the squat. A longer torso helps maintain an upright position. For example, two trainees of the same height, one with a long torso and short legs and the other with a short torso and long legs. A shorter torso can make it harder to stay upright. Conversely, a longer torso and short legs are an ideal body type for squatting. A challenging body structure for squatting is both a long femur or long legs and a short torso. In addition, there are also biomechanical differences between tall and short trainees. Taller lifters generally have more difficulty with the back squat than their shorter peers. Longer limb lengths, greater moment arm, longer distance of the bar and the like. What is the solution? First, widening the stance can change the anterior posterior positioning of the thighs, affecting the torso's position and angle relative to the thighs and creating shorter anterior posterior length during the squat. Another solution is heel elevation. Wearing weightlifting shoes with an elevated heel or placing small plates under the heels can improve squat depth and balance, particularly for individuals with limited ankle mobility or longer femurss. Another option is to shift the weight forward, such as in the front squat, allowing the trainee to maintain a more upright position. In the longer term, it is desirable to work on the mobility of the ankle joint that will allow the knees to slide forward without lifting the heels. In conclusion, the body's structure affects the ability to perform squats. Exercisers with a long thigh and a short torso will experience greater difficulty in staying upright. Listen to your body and use a variation that suits it. Whether it is widening your stance, raising the heels, improving mobility, or performing variations such as a front squat or goblet squat and the like. In this example, we can observe the contrast in proportions. Both are almost the same height, but the lengths of the femur and the torso are substantially different. A long femur and a short torso cause the trainee to lean forward. A short femur relative to a long tibia and a long torso facilitates a deep squat.

=== FILE: Brachial Plexus [rsCrP2vOnYk].en.txt ===

The Brachial Plexus The brachial plexus is a network of motor and sensory nerves that innervate all the muscles and skin in the upper limb. The brachial plexus is located between the neck and the axilla. This video will focus primarily on the motor nerve of the brachial plexus. The brachial plexus rises from the anterior rami of spinal nerve C5-T1, And merges and splits several times into trunks and divisions until they finally give rise to their terminal branches. The brachial plexus is divided into roots, trunks, divisions, cords and branches. Routes Let's start from the beginning at the vertebrae. Five roots rise from the anterior rami of vertebrae C5 to T1. The five roots merge to form three trunks:. The upper trunk (C5-C6). The middle trunk (C7). And the lower trunk (C8,T1). Divisions As the trunk reaches the posterior aspect of the middle third of the clavicle, It splits into the anterior and posterior divisions and forms six divisions: Three anterior divisions and three posterior divisions. The 6th division continues to pass inferiorly behind the clavicle, enters the axillary region, And becomes three cords. The three cords are named according to their location relative to the axillary artery. The three posterior divisions unite to form the posterior cord. The upper two anterior divisions unite to form the lateral cord. And the lower anterior division continues to form the medial cord. Terminal branches The cord split into five terminal branches at the inferior margin of the pectoralis minor. And you can use the acronyms "M-A-M-R-U" to remember them. The lateral cord splits into the musculocutaneous nerve (C5,C6 and C7). The posterior cord splits into the axillary nerve (C5 and C6). And into the radial nerve (C5,C6,C7,C8 and T1). The medial and lateral cords split into the median nerve (C5,C6,C7,C8 and T1). And into the ulnar nerve (C8 and T1). There are many more branches arising from different parts of the brachial plexus. The dorsal scapular nerve arises from root C5. The long thoracic nerve arises from roots, C5,C6 and C7. The upper trunk gives two branches that arise from roots C5 and C6, the nerve to the subclavius And the suprascapular nerve. The divisions do not provide any branches. The lateral pectoral nerves, C5,C6 and C7 arise from the lateral cord. The posterior cord gives branches to the upper subscapular nerve (C5,C6), The thoracodorsal nerve (C6, C7,C8), And the lower subscapular nerve (C5,C6). And the medial cord gives branches to the medial pectoral nerve (C8,T1). In summary, the brachial plexus is a network of five spinal nerves (C5 to T1). It is in charge of supplying sensory and motor innervation to the upper limb. And it is divided into trunks, divisions, cords and branches.

=== FILE: Chest Dips vs. Triceps Dips： Maximize Your Upper Body Training [5NciL-N2Qs4].txt ===

 chest-dips versus triceps-dips. Dips are a powerful upper-body exercise using your body weight for strength and muscle building. However, subtle adjustments in technique can help you target different muscles. It's not about isolating pecs or triceps. It's about customizing your hand placement and body angle for your desired goal. Let's examine the primary distinctions between these two variations of dips. Chest-dips. Hand positioning. Hands are set wider than shoulder width. Body alignment. The body is slightly tilted forward with the hips pushed back. Focus. The chest-dips emphasize shoulder dynamics over elbow movement. The forearm and bar are maintained at a larger angle. Triceps-dips. Hand positioning. Hands are kept at shoulder width. Body alignment. The torso is maintained upright. Focus. The triceps-dips emphasize vertical body movement through elbow flexion. The forearm and bar form a smaller angle.

=== FILE: Dumbbell Chest Press on Stability Ball ｜ Anatomical Analysis [upJyIg6ip3k].en.txt ===

Chest press stability ball. This exercise has two main aims. Active strengthening of the chest muscles and passive strengthening of the core muscles and buttocks. Sit on a stability ball. This video shows some variations. The aim of these exercises is to challenge the core muscles and strengthen the chest muscles. Note, because of the unstable base, we do not recommend working with heavy weights. It is preferable to work with heavy weights on a stable surface such as a bench. === FILE: Elbow Anatomy & Movement [c4B_4VMjlS4].en.txt ===

elbow joint anatomy and arthrokinematics the elbow joint though seemingly simple is a complex structure that allows forearm flexion extension and hand rotation to grasp its anatomy and functions let's break it down the important structures of the elbow can be divided into a few main categories bones and joints ligaments muscles and tendons the anatomy of the elbow joint the elbow joint enables various movements of the forearm while maintaining stability and protection of the bones involved the elbow joint is composed of three bones the humorous the alna and the radius Each of which contributes to specific aspects of elbow joint function let's delve into the details of each joint within the elbow the humo alner joint components this joint is formed by the articulation between the Trula of the humorus and the corresponding surface of the enna function the humo alna joint primarily enables flexion and extension of the elbow joint the humoral radial joint components the humo radial joint is created by the articulation between the the capitulum of the humorus and the head of the radius function the humal radial joint is responsible for pronation and supination of the forearm the proximal radio olner joint components the proximal radio olner joint is situated just below the radial head and is formed by the articulation between the radial Notch of the enna and the head of the radius function the primary function of the proximal radio alner joint is to allow for the rotation of the radius around the enna this rotation also facilitates superation and pronation movements of the forearm carrying angle the elbow joint has an important anatomical feature called the carrying angle or cupital angle formed between the humorous and the forearm axes when the arm is in a standard position with the Palm facing forward typically the carrying angle ranges from 5 to 15° it's generally greater in females 10 to 15° than in males 5 to 10° cupidus valgus deformity occurs when the forearm deviates laterally by more than 15° while cupidus Varys deformity is marked by a medial deviation of the forearm by less than 5° elbow ligaments the elbow stability and function rely heavily on a network of ligaments that offer support and restrict excessive movement the key elbow ligaments include the medial collateral ligament MCL found on the inner side of the elbow the MCL consists of three bands the anterior ligament the posterior ligament and the transverse ligament the medial collateral ligament primarily resists excessive vus outward stress injuries to the MCL are common in activities like throwing the lateral collateral ligament LCL located on the outer side of the elbow the LCL also called the radio colateral ligament RCL stabilizes against inward stress Varys preventing the elbow from opening on the outer side LCL injuries are less frequent but can occur due to traumatic events or dislocations the annular ligament the anular ligament encircles the radial head holding it firmly in place within the radial Notch of the alna this ligament allows for the smooth rotation of the radius during pronation and supination while maintaining stability the interus membrane the interus membrane positioned between the radius and the enna is vital for Distributing loads from the radius to the alna when bearing weight on the Upper Limb the orientation of its fibers facilitates efficient load transfer ensuring stability and balanced force distribution in the forearm and elbow during different activities the function of the elbow muscles elbow flexion during elbow flexion four distinct muscles come into play with their dominance dependent on the position of the hand ranging from superation to pronation the brachialis this muscle takes the lead when the arm is in a pronated position the bracho radialis becomes the dominant muscle when the hand is in a mid position the biceps brachi eye when the arm is in a supinated position the biceps brachy eye muscle becomes dominant it serves as the primary superator muscle and contributes significantly to Elbow flexion the pronator Terrace although its primary role is to assist in and turning the Palm downward pronation the pronator Terrace also lends a hand in elbow flexion elbow extension for straightening the elbow two key muscles come into play the triceps Breaky eye this muscle is the primary mover responsible for extending the elbow the anconeus despite being a small muscle the anconeus plays a role in both elbow extension and Joint stability elbow pronation when it comes to Elbow pronation two muscles work together the pronator Terrace this muscle takes the lead and the pronator quad ratus which works in conjunction with the pronator Terrace to facilitate pronation elbow supination elbow supination or the action of turning the Palm upward involves two key muscles the biceps brachi eye in addition to its role in elbow flexion the biceps brachi Eye plays a major part in turning the Palm upward supination and the supinator which assists the biceps brachi ey in forearm supination these muscles work together seamlessly to control the diverse movements of the elbow joint allowing for the flexion the extension and the rotational movements needed for various everyday tasks

=== FILE: Everything You Need to Know to Master the Headstand Pose [AY6Cj-S7uO0].en.txt ===

Headstand pose. Shir chassana. The headstand is one of the popular asinas in yoga and the ability to hold the position must be developed slowly and gradually in order to balance the body when it is upside down. Active maintenance of a strong body center is necessary as well as the kinesthetic ability to perceive the precise position of one's body limbs in space. The upper trapezius elevates the scapula and rotates it outward with the help of the serrus anterior and lower trapezius. The deltoid flexes and stabilizes the arm. Problem one. Because body weight is placed on the top of the head and the forearm, the tendency is to create excess load on the cervical vertebrae. Solution: During instruction, emphasize balanced distribution of body weight on the forearms. Problem two, lack of balance and accompanying movements. Solution, perform the position near the wall. Problem three, weakness in the shoulder girdle limits the ability to stabilize the scapula and to push the shoulders down towards the floor. The main active muscles are upper trapezius, deltoid, triceps, brachi, and serratus anterior. Solution preparatory exercises for strengthening these muscles. The general instruction should be to distribute the load between the head and the hands. Legs on table, trunk bent, most of the body weight rests on the table, low load on the head and hands. Legs on table, trunk straight, greater load on the shoulder girdle. Placing flexed legs on a stool. You can play with the load on the stool. One leg in the air. Greater load on the upper part of the body.

=== FILE: Hip Thrust vs. RDL： Which is Best for Your Glutes？ [GQXIPXBNQhA].en.txt ===

Romanian deadlift versus hip thrust which is best for your glutes starting with the Romanian deadlift or RDL this movement stretches the glutes as you bend forward reaching maximum contraction when they're in a lengthened position however standing upright in the shortened position puts less load on the glutes in contrast the hip thrust activates the glutes most intensely at the top of the movement reaching maximum activation in a shortened position in the lengthened position although the external load is still high the glutes are less active due to the involvement of other supporting muscles and passive assistance so which one should you choose the answer is both by combining rdls and hip thrusts you can ensure full range glute development optimizing strength and muscle growth for the best possible results

=== FILE: Hip Thrust vs. RDL： Which is Best for Your Glutes？ 🍑💪 [NFOaPMzx8-4].en.txt ===

Romanian deadlift versus hip thrust. Which is best for your glutes? Starting with the Romanian deadlift or RDL. This movement stretches the glutes as you bend forward, reaching maximum contraction when they're in a lengthened position. However, standing upright in the shortened position puts less load on the glutes. In contrast, the hip thrust activates the glutes most intensely at the top of the movement, reaching maximum activation in a shortened position. In the lengthened position, although the external load is still high, the glutes are less active due to the involvement of other supporting muscles and passive assistance. So, which one should you choose? The answer is both. By combining RDL's and hip thrusts, you can ensure full range glute development, optimizing strength and muscle growth for the best possible results.

in healthy back cases such as in tying shoes picking a light object off the ground etc in many daily activities that are not involved with higher vertical pressure rounding the spine comes naturally === FILE: How to Burpee ｜ Watch all active muscles [r6MBg8-FlD4].en.txt ===

=== FILE: How to Sumo Deadlift Correctly - Avoid Common Mistakes ｜ 3D Anatomical Analysis [TrNEnQ5XCBE].en.txt ===

sumo deadlift take your stance feet under bar with a wide stance and your toes out squat down and grasp bar squeeze the bar off the floor and drag it up your legs until it locks out of the top do not pull back into the shins the bar should come off the ground in a vertical path keep these three rules 1 the bar must start over mid foot to the shoulders must start slightly in front of the bar 3 the hips must start relatively high the target muscles are the quadriceps femoris and gluteus maximus colored red note also the spinal erectors can be a part of the target muscles isometric contraction the action in the hip joint is extension with adduction the adductors adduct the hip and assist in hip extension keep your feet between 30 to 45 degrees out keep your knees out in line with your toes and make sure you fully extend at the top overhand grip versus mixed grip overhand grip is a pronated grip with both palms facing you one of the benefits of overhand grip is that it allows you to keep the bar close to your body easily the mixed grip features one hand in a pronated position and the other in a separated position in other words one palm faces you and the other faces away the grip generally allows people to lift more weight however the mixed deadlift grip creates a higher potential for injuries such as bicep tears hold your knees out with wide stance push knees out to the sides and not down to prevent knees from buckling inward pushing knees out will allow hips to travel toward bar more quickly while improving leverage common mistake spine is not neutral it is very common to see both sides of the spectrum when it comes to not having a neutral spine on one hand we have the athlete whose spine is in flexion rounded on the other hand we have the athlete who over pants aids and pulls the lower lumbar into hyperextension creating an extreme amount of tension and pressure on that area common mistake the bar is far from the tibia the bar must start over mid foot

only jump squat jump where the athlete gets into a half squat position or half deadlift position then pauses and performs only the pushing up phase of the jump it's an explosive movement but not === FILE: Improve Your Squat ｜ Tripod stand [A4M39vPEMm8].en.txt ===

the foot tripod concept is a useful way of thinking about how to evenly distribute weight on the foot the tripod refers to three points of contact that the bottom of the foot makes with the ground the three points are one center of the calcaneus heel to head of the fifth metatarsal little toe 3 a head of the first metatarsal big toe done correctly there should be pressure on all three aspects of the tripod foot tighten the arch by pressing the toes down and back without shifting the weight out of the heel a stable base will balance the load on all the joints and bones the principle is to statically activate the foot muscles in order to maintain the arch and at the same time to activate the hip rotators by rotating the hip outward it is necessary to anchor the knee and hip as well therefore at the same time we'll drive the knee outward hip external rotation rooting is about generating tension through the feet that transfers through the whole body and locks you into the ground

=== FILE: Incline Shoulder Press Smith Machine： Technique, Muscles, and Key Mistakes [dRF2f5vsUXQ].txt ===

 inclined shoulder press, Smith machine. The inclined shoulder press on the Smith machine, performed at a 60-degree angle, closely resembles a traditional shoulder press, but also involves the upper chest due to the inclined position. Lie on an inclined bench with your back against the bar, with the bar aligned just below your shoulders. Ensure that your feet are flat on the floor. Grab the bar with an overhand grip. Your hands should be slightly wider than shoulder width apart. Elbows fully extended. Take a deep breath and lift the bar off the hooks. The bar should be positioned slightly below shoulder level. Slowly lower the bar until it touches your upper chest. Your elbows should point slightly downward to the sides. Push the bar back up to the starting position. Repeat the exercise for the desired number of repetitions. The movement in the shoulder joint is abduction with slight horizontal abduction. The deltoid abducks the shoulder with the assistance of the upper pectoralis, clavicular head. The triceps brachii extend the elbow. The abdominals and pelvic floor muscles stabilize the spine. Common mistake, setting the bench angle too low during an inclined press. A low bench angle makes the movement more chest dominant and reduces the emphasis on the anterior deltoid. To correct this, increase the incline to about 60 degrees to better target the anterior deltoid and ensure more effective muscle engagement in the upper portion of the press.

=== FILE: Leg Tuck (LTK) Anatomical & Kinesiological analysis [g1ixrBiqH6Q].en.txt ===

leg tuck complete as many leg tucks as possible maintain a relative vertical posture while moving the hips and knees up and down without excessive swinging hold the bar with an alternating grip legs off the floor from a straight arm position pull the body up until the knees touch the elbows return to the starting straight arm the latissimus dorsi extends the arm the abdominal muscles stabilize the pelvis and flex the spine the hip flexors pull the legs the biceps brachii brachialis and brachioradialis flex the elbow this exercise is considered difficult for several reasons the first reason is that the movement involves many joints and muscles the second reason for the difficulty in performing the exercise is the heavy load on the abdominal muscles on the one hand the abdominal muscles flex the spine and they also stabilize the pelvis raising the knees activates the hip flexors which pull the pelvis and tilt it anteriorly in addition pulling the body up activates the latissimus dorsi which also pulls the pelvis and creates anterior pelvic tilt in other words the abdominal muscles must be strong in order to stabilize the pelvis and also flex the spine note that this exercise requires normative spinal flexion ability normative means uniform integrative flexion of the vertebrae and creation of a sequential arch in this test of seated flexion we see a deficient sequential arch in the lumbar vertebrae which is usually caused by structural rigidity or shortened muscles such as in the quadratus lumborum this deficiency does not allow exercisers to flex the spine and bring the knees to reach the elbows even if the muscles are very strong

=== FILE: Lizard Pose： Achieve Optimal Hip Mobility [14rq7Gfm4aM].en.txt ===

utan prasana the lizard pose is a powerful hip opener that stretches the hip flexors on one hip and the hip extensors on the other start in a downward dog then step your leg forward and place the foot at your hand line either on the outside of the hand or between both hands and then reposition the hands your front knee should be bent with your toes and hip turned out to the side your back leg can be straight or bent depending on your preference keep the elbows at the shoulder line with the forearms and fingers spread on the mat maintain a neutral neck position with gaze focused toward the ground the movement in the hip joint of the forward leg is flexion in a chain reaction hip flexion tilts the pelvis posteriorly and flexes the lumbar spine the muscles stretched in the forward leg are gluteus maximus hamstrings Adu s the quadratus lumborum is stretched with flexion in the lumbar spine part of the hip Rotator group among them the quadratus femoris is stretched with hip flexion the posterior ligaments surrounding the hip joint are stretched in the rear leg hip extension stretches the iliopsoas muscles that connect the hip to the pelvis and the spine in addition to hip extension posterior pelvic tilt adds more stretch to this muscle another variation for performing the Asana performing the pose with straightened rear knee in the air and Toes digging into the ground this variation of the pose is more active mainly in regard to the rear leg as the toes dig into the floor the gluteus maximus of the forward leg is dominantly active in supporting the body the hip flexors illiop sois rectus femoris are stretched and active simultaneously the quadricep stabilize the body this muscle is active in both legs the hip adductors are stretched and active simultaneously to stabilize the body common problems and adaptations common problem cervical tension caused by raising the head solution turn the Gaze downward and maintain the cervical curve in a neutral position it is possible to use visualization imagine holding a small ball between the chin and chest and not letting it fall common problem difficulty in attaining the initial stage of the pose in leaning the forward foot forward with full range of motion because of shortened hamstrings Andor gluteus maximus the assana is entered from the dog pose with both hands on the floor from this position it is difficult to transfer the foot forward between the arms moving the foot forward requires bringing the thigh to the chest hip flexion stretches the gluteus maximus and the hamstrings in addition knee extension is required to allow the foot to move forward tight hamstrings will pull the lower leg backward and not allow the knee to straighten and shortened hamstrings will not allow full knee flexion solution a enter the position from a standing pose instead of from the dog pose from standing taking the leg one step backward squat movement makes a greater range of motion possible B reach the position from a starting position on hands and knees C use the hand to help place the foot in the same line as the hands common problem difficulty in attaining the initial stage of the pose in leaning the foot forward with full range of motion because of a limitation in the range of ankle dorsiflexion and shortening of the plantar flexor muscles the ASA is entered from the dog pose to move the foot forward between the arms the exerciser must bring the knee to the chest and perform ankle dorsal flexions so that the foot does not get stuck on the floor many exercisers have difficulty in performing this ankle dorsiflexion because of limited range of motion Andor plantar flexion muscles in order to perform ankle dorsiflexion the dorsal flexors become active and on the other side the plantar flexors are actively stretched a shortening in these muscles will not allow exercises to perform perform full motion dors of flexion and the foot will get stuck on the floor solution perform a variation beginning from a full standing position while standing one leg is Led backwards as in a backward squat in this variation two the forward foot is in dors of flexion but this time it is the result of passive stretching of the plantar flexors the dorsal flexors do not have to strain to move the ankle therefore it will be easier to attain the range required for the position common problem difficulty in leading the forward leg forward with full range because of weakness of the scapular protractors entering the Asana begins with a dog pose in order to transfer the leg forward between the arms the exerciser must push the chest upward in order to make room for the leg the chest is pushed mainly by protracting the scapula using the seratus an interior muscle weakness in this muscle leads to sinking of the chest and lack of room for transferring the leg solution Preparatory exercise to strengthen the seratus anterior muscle placing the hands on an exercise block or two common problem limited range of flexion in the lower vertebrae that limits the ability of the forarms to reach the floor solution a place the hands on an exercise block or two adjust the block height to the exerciser range of flexion with Improvement it will be possible to place the forearms on the blocks second picture common problem shortening of the flexion muscles in the rear leg limits the range of movement in leading the leg forward it reduces the length of the step why does this happen shortening of the iliopsoas muscle in the rear leg pulls and rotates the pelvis anteriorly and does not allow it to rotate to the rear lack of posterior rotation does not allow the forward leg to perform the full flexion and bring the knee to the chest as full hip flexion is dependent on posterior pelvic rotation one side affects the other solution support the hands on two blocks and reduce the distance between the legs

=== FILE: Master Frog Pose： Open Hips & Avoid Mistakes [vDC0FoknW-w].en.txt ===

the Frog pose is a popular yoga pose aiding in releasing tension points and improving hip movement in the groin and hip joints from a seated position on the heels transition into a tabletop position with your arms align shoulder width apart and your knees at hip width from tabletop slowly widen your knees apart until you feel a stretch in the inner thigh muscles or in the groin the feet move into dors of flexion and open outward knees and ankles are both at 90 degrees angles with the inner part of the knees and the inner part of the soles of the feet touching the ground from this position gently lower your hips and place your palms on the ground keeping your hands firmly rooted on the mat fingertips spread wide for support or parallel to each other relax your upper body and gently move the head forward and the tailbone towards the opposite direction from this position a gentle backward movement in intensifies the stretch gradually release tension in the neck and let your chest sink downwards according to your own range of motion stay in this position for several long and relaxed breaths while being attuned to your body to exit the Frog pose gradually and slowly return the knees and the soles of the feet to the starting position in the center line on the hands and knees and into a tabletop position from tabletop return to a seated position on the heels with soles of the feet in plantar flexion the spine is in a neutral position the pelvis is in a neutral position the hip joint is in horizontal abduction which is a combination of flexion plus hip abduction there is no external rotation in the hips only the horizontal Abduction the knee joint is in external rotation the ankle joint is in doors of flexion The Joint in which rotation occurs is the knee joint in which external rotation occurs notice how the rotation in the knee rotates the ankle although the ankle rotates the movement comes from the knee note that excessive rotation of the knee can lead to potential knee injuries to prevent knee injuries refer to common problems the hip adductors are stretched rotating the foot outward will intensify the stretch of the hip adductors this may be attributed to the gillus muscle which is connected to the tibia bone as the tibia rotates the gillus is stretched even more creating an intense stretching sensation common problem discomfort or pain in the medial aspect of the knee joints due to direct pressure between the knee and the floor solution use padding to create cushioning under the knees common problem Limited range of motion and horizontal abduction in the hip joint which requires increased rotation in the knee joint causing knee pain from a Kinesiology perspective the knee joint mainly moves in the sagittal plane flexion and extension but when the knee is bent rotation can also be performed which is important in Daily functions the normal rotation range when the knee is flexed is 40° however there is a greater risk of knee injury if excessive rotation occurs in situations where hip range of motion is limited this combination of stiffness and limited range of motion in hip abduction with strong rotation in the knees as a result is precisely the challenging aspect of the Frog pose if not performed correctly the sensation of stretching should be felt in the groin area and the muscles surrounding the hip if the sensation moves to the knee practitioners must exit the Frog pose applying force or pressure on the knee joints can cause serious damage to the meniscus and the supporting ligaments of the knee joints practitioners with a suitable natural structure and high flexibility in the hip joints can perform the pose relatively easily since their hip abduction range is good however for many practitioners the structure of the groin and hip joint does not allow the hips to achieve full abduction resulting in strong rotational stress on the knee if practitioners push the body downward forcefully the the hip and thigh bones act as two levers creating a strong rotational force on the knee never push a student to perform or stay in this position if they feel sensitivity or pain in the knee joint Solutions a perform the pose without dorsal flexion in the ankles meaning in a plantar flexion position in the ankles in this position there is no rotation in the knees if hip abduction range is limited and pressure is exerted on the knees it is important to keep the ankles in a plantar flexion position dorsal flexion in the ankles will cause rotation in the knees leading to pain B Elevate the buttocks by placing yoga blocks under the elbows to reduce the vertical pressure and decrease the intensity of the stretch C perform a variation of the pose with the hands stretched forward without knee rotation D perform a restorative variation of the Frog pose with full support under the body using a bolster this allows for a more restorative pose with full relaxation of all body muscles

=== FILE: Master Reverse Frog Hypers： Build Glutes & Core Stability [R9AILoVT3mo].en.txt ===

Reverse frog hypers. This exercise targets the glutius maximus and erector spina. Lie face down on a bench. Chest and lower abdomen on the bench and legs off the bench. Bring the feet together and abduct the knees froglike. Then raise the legs. Extend the hip and lower them back down. The glutius maximus extends the hip joint. The leg position creates an external rotation of the hip which recruits the glutius maximus muscle more. The main stabilizers are the spinal erectors and latisimus dorsy. The lumbar erectors may also contract a little dynamically depending on the pelvic movement. The more the pelvis moves between a anterior pelvic tilt and ppt posterior pelvic tilt, the more active they will be. The elbow flexors stabilize the elbow. Common mistake, overextension of the lumbar spine while raising the legs. Usually because of uncontrolled momentum. To prevent the extension, instruct the trainee to slightly contract the abdominal muscles while lifting the legs.

=== FILE: Mastering Balance： The Science of Center of Gravity and Base of Support [5s1gEi5wfLg].en.txt ===

center of gravity and base of support have you ever wondered how we maintain balance while walking running or simply standing still our ability to maintain balance or stability is primarily dependent on the relationship between the body's center of gravity at its base of support let's dive into these two concepts the center of gravity Cog the center of gravity cogg is a point in space where the entire body's Mass behaves as if it is is concentrated there the center of gravity varies from person to person but on average when standing upright it is located in the body midline in front of the lumbar vertebrae L1 2 3 however this point can shift depending on an individual's body shape posture and position for instance when you bend forward or backward your center of gravity moves accordingly the base of support BOS the body's base of support BOS refers to the area or part of the body that is in contact with the supporting surface such as the ground or a floor were you to trace the outline of the body where it touches the ground you would Define the base of support it is the foundation that provides stability and balance to the body when standing or performing various movements for example The Wider The Stance when standing the larger the base of support becomes whereas a narrow stance reduces the size of the base of support this is why it's challenging to maintain weight distribution when standing with feet close together as it requires keeping the center of gravity on a small support base what happens when we lose balance losing balance occurs when the body center of gravity moves outside the base of support which significantly affects the ability to maintain balance when this happens two options arise either falling to the ground or taking a step forward to increase the base of support low center of gravity versus high center of gravity another crucial Factor influencing balance is the distance between your center of gravity and your base of support the rule here is simple the lower your center of gravity the more stable you are and conversely the higher your center of gravity is the less stable you are and the easier it is to topple over if pushed low center of gravity sumo wrestler in sumo wrestling wrestlers purposefully lower their center of gravity by adopting a deep wide-legged stance this low center of gravity and increased base of support provide exceptional stability making it incredibly challenging for opponents to push and topple them over during a match High center of gravity tiptoe balancing when balancing on tiptoes the center of GRA gravity is much higher above the ground making it prone to instability maintaining stability requires constant effort and control balancing on tiptoes vividly demonstrates how a high center of gravity increases instability and the risk of toppling over compared to a lower center of gravity stance

=== FILE: Mastering the Zercher Squat： Enhance Strength with Perfect Form [gAC8RsZ9RpA].en.txt ===

zercher squat the zercher squat is named after the weightlifter Ed zercher in this squat variation we place the barbell in the elbows crease placing the barbell in front of the body creates a relatively large rotational movement and pulls the body forward this allows the trainee to lean back slightly and keep the body almost completely vertical to the floor the disadvantage of this exercise is the discomfort that can be created in the elbow crease when working with heavy weights the bar can be padded with a towel or sponge or the trainee can wear a long sleeved shirt the active joints are the knee hip and Ankle joints the quadriceps femoris extends the knees the gluteus maximus extends the hip with the hamstrings assistance the gastric nemus and Solus plantar flexes the ankle joint the erector spine a along with the core muscles stabilize the Torso pulling the barbell for forward allows the trainee to lean forward slightly and to lower the body with relatively little dorsa flexion in the ankle this is not possible in the back squat which requires a greater range of movement in the ankle there are two usual ways to place the dumbbell on the crook of the elbows one begin with the barbell on the floor deadlift the bar to your thigh height and rest it on your thighs scoop your arms underneath the bar and you can rested on your elbow crease note this variation isn't suitable for heavy weight at the end of the exercise place the bar on the thighs release the arms hold the bar and return it to the floor while keeping your back straight two begin the exercise with the bar placed on a power rack A variation with a kettle bell hanging in the center of a body bar the body bar is padded and relatively thick which might be a better option into place in the elbow crease

=== FILE: Maximize Posterior Deltoid Gains with the Machine Reverse Fly [bKu9Ys175VY].en.txt ===

reverse fly machine the machine reverse fly is an exercise intended to strengthen your posterior deltoid sit on a reverse flying machine and hold the handles with your hands your arms should be parallel to the ground and your elbows should be slightly flexed horizontally abduct your shoulders without flexing or extending your elbows go back to the starting position and repeat the posterior deltoid horizontally abducts the shoulder with the assistance of the infraspinatus the trapezius and the rhomboids assist by retracting the scapula the triceps brachi ey stabilize the elbow joint common mistake moving your elbows when performing the reverse flies this exercise is intended to strengthen your posterior deltoid by horizontally abducting your shoulder doing this exercise with a flexed elbow will decrease the torque arm on the posterior deltoid to focus the load on the posterior deltoid decrease the resistance then do the exercise again and see if you can do it with your elbows straight common mistake rotating your spine rotating your spine during the exercise will help create momentum to pull the weight and will decrease the load on the posterior deltoid which will not serve the purpose of the exercise

=== FILE: Maximize your vertical jump with Depth Jumps [NvSkuYwNxco].en.txt ===

depth jump the depth jump is a plyometric exercise which can be performed to improve reactive and eccentric strength stand on a low box step off the box and land on the ground with both feet as soon as you touch the ground jump up to the higher box use this plyometric exercise to improve the explosive and reactive properties of your legs reactive jump demonstrates an athletes ability to rapidly change from an eccentric motion into a concentric muscular contraction and is an expression of their Dynamic explosive vertical jump capacity the target muscles are the quadriceps femoris Gastro nemus and glutitis maximus muscles which are essential for jumping the use of gravity to load the lower extremities takes advantage of the elastic properties of the lower legs and feet this action is accompanied by the strength and power of the quadriceps and gluto muscles to produce strong extension at the knees and hips the erector spiny along with the other core muscles in light brown stabilize the spine arms swing helps to increase vertical jump height the anterior deltoid flexes the shoulder raises the arm the arm swings contribute to the enhancement of the jump height not only by raising the center of gravity of the arms but by augmenting the work of the lower extremity joints especially of the hip and Ankle joints concentric jump versus plyometric jump on the left we can see an example of a concentric only jump squat jump where the athlete gets into a half squat position or half deadlift position then pauses and performs only the pushing up phase of the jump it's an explosive movement but not considered as a a plyometric movement on the right we see a plyometric jump this exercise is a slightly more advanced exercise than the counter movement jump stand on the box step off as if you were falling to the floor and as soon as you touch the ground jump up rebounding such a movement makes use of the stretch shorten cycle where the muscles are pre-stretched before shortening in the desired Direction

=== FILE: Medial Collateral Ligament (MCL) #mcl [KCxBsMF_29s].en.txt ===

the medial collateral ligament MCL found on the inner side of the elbow the MCL consists of three bands the anterior ligament the posterior ligament and the transverse ligament the medial collateral ligament primarily resists excessive vus outward stress injuries to the MCL are common in activities like throwing

=== FILE: Medicine Ball Wall I Anatomical Analysis [Il6E_ExCexU].en.txt ===

the target muscles are the gluteus maximus quadriceps femoris and anterior deltoid colored in red throwing the ball overhead against a wall requires explosive power that begins in the legs passes through the shoulder joints and elbows and the hand it is important to maintain a neutral spine throughout the movement common mistake standing too far from the wall will not allow you to catch the ball as it returns from the wall common mistake lumbar flexion and or thorax flexion

=== FILE: Meet the gluteus maximus - the largest muscle in your body! [xBQdEgRKVzg].en.txt ===

this is the gluteus maximus the largest muscle in our body and you know what they say with great power comes great responsibility it starts at the sacrum and ilium and attaches to the femur and the ITB which means it also affects the knee the main function of the gluteous Maximus is hip extension in simple words it pushes your leg back or if your foot is on the ground it straighten your body it also performs external rotation at the hip and because of its connection to the ITB it assists with lateral rotation at the knee you you can see that its fibers are angled diagonally giving it even more functions when it works on one side it rotates the pelvis and when both sides contract it performs posterior pelvic tilt additionally due to the angles of its connections the upper fibers create Abduction of the hip while the lower fibers create adduction and together it stabilizes the pelvis while lifting the opposite leg any movement involving this actions will train the glus Maximus but if you want to keep it simple squats and deadlifts will do the trick

=== FILE: Peacock Pose Mayurasana Explained [-5113Q1KEDQ].txt ===

 peacock-pose, mayurasana, is an advanced yoga posture that balances the body on the hands with the elbows pressing into the abdomen. It demands strength, focus, and flexibility. Begin in Vajrasana, sitting on your heels. Place your hands on the floor in front of you, fingers pointing back toward the body. Separate the knees slightly and press the elbows into the abdomen on either side of the navel. Lean forward, rest the chest on the elbows, and extend both legs straight back, keeping them together with the toes on the floor. Shift your weight forward and lift the legs, balancing the body and the air on your forearms. Engage the core, keep the back straight, engage forward. Maintain smooth, steady breathing, then gently lower the legs and return to your seated position. In the peacock pose, the body relies on coordinated muscular engagement and precise joint positioning to maintain balance and lift. The lumbar spinal vertebrae are in extension, the thoracic spinal vertebrae are in slight flexion, and the cervical vertebrae are in extension. The erector spanny muscles are active, while the abdominal muscles provide core stability, helping to maintain balance and support the pressure applied to the abdomen by the elbows. The shoulder joint is inflection, external rotation, and adduction. Both the anterior deltoid and pectoralis major are actively working. The elbow joint shifts between flexion and extension. It's a balancing point, a kind of seesaw between the two positions. Depending on how the body's weight moves forward or backward, the triceps brachy and elbow flexors act as synergists, supporting the pose by bearing the body's weight and providing stability to the wrist joints. The triceps brachy and elbow flexors act as synergists, supporting the pose by bearing the body's weight and providing stability to the wrist joints. The wrist joint is in dorsiflexion, while the wrist and finger flexor muscles are stretched. The scapular joint is in protraction. The serratus anterior and rotator cuff muscles act as synergists, working to stabilize the shoulders and support the lifting of the body. The hips are extended. The glutus maximus is active, while the quadriceps femoris and hamstrings support and stabilize the pose. The gluteus maximus is active, while the triceps and the gluteus maximus are extended. Common problem, difficulty maintaining stability in the air, often caused by weakness in the core, especially the abdominal muscles and lower back extensors. To improve stability, try the following modifications. Place two yoga blocks under the feet and shin for added support, or lift only one leg off the ground to reduce the challenge. You can also support the abdomen with a block or small bench, keeping the elbows beside the body rather than directly beneath it. Using a bolster between the arms and abdomen can also help. With this support, explore variations such as lifting one leg at a time, or resting the forehead on the mat while raising both legs. Common problem, limited wrist extension causes pain and overload on the wrist bones. About 90 degrees of wrist extension is needed for safe weight bearing. Without it, forcing the position can strain or injure the base of the palms. Before practicing, bring the forearms and hands together, open the elbows and lower the hands. If the palms separate before 90 degrees, mobility is limited. If they stay together, the range is sufficient. Modification option, adjust hand placement by turning the fingers outward instead of inward to reduce pressure on the wrists. To build safe foundations, use preparatory exercises such as dorsiflexion stretches, wrist flexor stretches, finger palm flexion extension, and wrist flexion on blocks. Always work with control and proper technique. Common problem, incorrect elbow placement. Solution 1, enter the pose with initial support under the pelvis and elevate the hands by placing a yoga block beneath them. Solution 2, use a resistance band around the elbows for additional support. Common problem, fear of falling forward. Since the pose requires lifting the entire body, some students feel anxious about losing balance and falling. Solution A, place a cushion or bolster under the face for safety. Solution B, practice the pose without lifting the legs off the mat.

=== FILE: Pigeon Pose ｜ Full Anatomical Analysis + Mistakes to Avoid [0NvqT1j3000].en.txt ===

pigeon pose ekapata rajakapatasana the pigeon pose is one of the most popular yoga poses and it helps release tension points and improve the range of motion in the hip joints this pose requires particularly high mobility in the hip joint practitioners with a suitable natural structure and unusual flexibility can do it relatively easily still in most cases the hip joint structure does not allow the femur to use the entire rotation range and creates a break unsuitable or too aggressive practice can cause severe damage to the knee joint as will be presented in the kinesiological analysis of this pose the correct way to perform the pigeon pose step by step start on all fours and bring one leg forward with the knee between your hands the front knee is bent in the sagittal plane take the foot away from the thigh to open the knee angle while keeping the pelvis centered notice how it creates rotation in the hip and knee joints the back leg is straight in plantar flexion make sure to maintain the pelvis in the center inhale in this position while the chest faces forward and upward turning the sternum flashlight around the hands are placed on the floor to the sides of the knee inhale with the chest facing forward and upward the center of the chest bone faces forward exhale and descend slightly with the pelvis in the center without collapsing to one side after a few breaths slide the hands forward on the floor and rest your forehead between the hands or on the palms the movement in the hip integrates flexion and external rotation pay attention to the tension created in the lateral band of the ilia femoral ligament y ligament the stretched muscles are gluteus medius piriformis gluteus minimus gluteus maximus during elevation and thoracic extension the back erectors contract and the iliopsoas muscles of the back leg are stretched here's one common problem when performing this pose increased rotation in the front knee joint resulting from limited external rotation in the hip joint the reason for that could be shortened internal rotators or limited mobility in the ligaments around the hip joint with an emphasis on the lateral portion practitioners with a suitable natural structure and high flexibility in the hip joints can perform the full pose relatively easily as their thighs can rotate outward fully for many practitioners the hip joint structure disables the femur's range of rotation and a chain reaction of excessively strong rotation is created in the knee if the student or teacher forcefully pushes the body down the tibia and femur will serve as two levers creating large rotational force on the knee therefore there is an actual risk of knee injury when a combination of three components is created at the same time a flexion b rotation c load this combination is exactly the danger of the pigeon pose if it is performed incorrectly the practitioner should feel the stretch only in the hip joint if the stretch moves to the knee you should exit the pose immediately but carefully pressing the legs while applying force into the position is one of the most dangerous things in the pigeon pose and can lead to severe injury in the supporting ligaments of the knee joints never force or pressure a student to perform or stay in this position if they feel tenderness or pain in the knee joint in any case of knee pain do not perform this pose at all a possible solution for this problem each practitioner has a different range of motion in the hip joint and the pose needs to be adapted personally according to this range there are a few possible solutions in case of knee pain in the pigeon pose a reduce the angle between the knee and thigh to avoid rotation as the angle increases so does rotation in the knee b choose an alternative exercise to improve flexibility and mobility of the hip joint the following is a recommended variation using the wall to improve flexibility and mobility of the hip joint the following is a recommended variation using the wall to improve hip rotator flexibility c in any case of limited lateral rotation in the hip or sensitivity in the knee use adaptations to maintain a balanced position of the pelvis for example sitting on a block or a bolster to create elevation on one side of the pelvis d when the pigeon pose is performed fully the front ankle should be in dorsiflexion however if the thigh rotation is limited and there is still pressure on the knee it is essential to keep the knee joint in the sagittal plane and the ankle in plantar flexion in this position even though the knee is bent the dorsiflexion in the ankle creates rotation and a risk to the knee although the knee is in flexion in this position the ankle is in dorsiflexion causing rotation and endangering the knee in this position it is best to keep the angle in plantar flexion plantar flexion in the ankle is more suitable as it decreases rotation in the knee joint and reduces the risk of injury the second problem in this exercise is sensitivity or pain in the lower back as a result of strong arching increased lumbar lordosis the higher the arms and the closer to the knee the deeper the arch in the lower back in cases of tenderness in the lower back lordosis etc it is advised to move the palms forward slightly to reduce the degree of lumbar lordosis additional adaptations laying the forehead on a block with hand support in this condition lumbar lordosis is eliminated

=== FILE: Planes of movement [r0pVf60fTIA].en.txt ===

planes of movement human movements are described in terms of the plane in which they pass through there are three planes of motion the frontal plane the sagittal plane the transverse or horizontal plane the frontal plane passes through the body from left to right dividing the body into anterior and posterior parts frontal plane movements are lateral side to side movements for example abduction and adduction lateral flexion of the spine foot inversion and eversion wrist radial and ulnar deviation and scapular elevation and depression the sagittal plane passes through the body from front to back dividing the body into left and right parts sagittal plane movements are forward and backward movements for example flexion extension shoulder flexion and extension elbow flexion and extension spinal flexion and extension ankle dorsiflexion and plantar flexion the transverse or horizontal plane passes through the body in a line parallel to the floor dividing the body into top and bottom parts transverse plane movements are twisting or rotational movements for example horizontal adduction and abduction or horizontal flexion and extension spinal rotation internal and external rotation pronation and supination here are some demonstrations of strength exercises in the various movement planes deepen your understanding of the human body check out our apps sign up for free

=== FILE: Push-Ups Common Mistakes [6SkKPgKX_gY].en.txt ===

Push-ups. Common mistakes. Push-ups are one of the most popular strength training exercises. We do them mainly to strengthen the chest and core muscles. But what happens when the chest muscles don't have enough strength or there is a lack of awareness of the correct movement? One of the common mistakes is that the up and down movement is performed through adduction and abduction of the scapula. The main muscle that abducts the scapula is the anterior serratus in red and not the pectoral muscles. Note that the arms remain almost straight throughout the movement. So what is the correct movement? We bring the chest to the floor as we flex the elbows and we maintain the spinal curves in neutral position as in standing. Below we see the common mistake of straight arms and movement through the scapula. Above in the upper animation we see the correct performance. So how do we solve the problem? If you don't have enough chest muscle strength, simply place your knees on the floor. This reduces the load and enables you to perform the movement correctly. As you progress, you can then perform the exercise with legs straight. In both variations, be sure to maintain a straight back, neutral curves. Thanks for watching. We hope you enjoyed it.

=== FILE: Radial Nerve： Anatomy & Function [46ATiLcWwlA].txt ===

 radial nerve. The radial nerve arises from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus, with root contributions from C5 to T1. It travels posterior to the axillary artery, from where it gives motor branches to the long head and lateral head of the triceps brachia, and then enters the arm through the triangular interval. From there, it descends in the radial groove of the humerus, supplying the medial head of the triceps brachii and the enconius, then pierces the lateral intermuscular septum to enter the anterior compartment, running between the brachialis and brachioradialis. At the level of the lateral epicondyle, it divides into two terminal branches. The deep branch, also called the posterior innerosseus nerve, passes through and innervates the supinator muscle. From there, it continues distally to supply all the extensor muscles of the forearm, including the extensor polysis brevis, extensor indusis, extensor polysis longus, abductor polysis longus, extensor carpi radialis brevis, extensor carpi ulnaris, extensor digitai minimi, extensor digitai minimi, extensor digitai minimi, and the extensor digitorum. The superficial branch continues beneath the brachioradialis to provide cutaneous sensation to the dorsum of the hand. The posterior cutaneous nerve is used to create a more elastic and elastic effect. Additional sensory branches include the posterior cutaneous nerve of the arm, the posterior cutaneous nerve of the forearm, and the lower lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm. These branches supply sensation to the posterior arm, posterior forearm, and lateral aspect of the distal upper arm. In summary, the radial nerve provides motor innervation to the triceps brachii, the anconius, and all the extensor muscles of the forearm. It also supplies sensory innervation to the skin of the lateral aspect of the arm, posterior arm, the posterior forearm, and the dorsum of the lateral hand, including the dorsal aspects of the thumb, index, middle finger, and the radial half of the ring finger.

=== FILE: Running Anatomy, Elastic Recoil [ndGUNOfS3t0].en.txt ===

in classic muscle training slow contraction the muscle fibers actively change in length while the tendons scarcely grow larger however during walking running or hopping the muscle fibers contract almost isometrically well the tendons lengthen and shorten like an elastic yo-yo spring the tendons stretch and recoil storing and retaining elastic strain energy the Achilles tendon stretches when the foot hits the ground in the body sinks as it is lowered and all this stored energy is released like a spring during the toe off phase this is the body's way of conserving metabolic energy

=== FILE: Seated Cable Row Locked Torso or Moving Torso 🎥 [m1tPzavzegM].en.txt ===

=== FILE: Short vs. Long Step Lunges–What’s the Difference？ 🔥 [mi4feuUCU-I].en.txt ===

Short versus long step lunges. What's the difference? The length of your step in a lunge determines which muscles do the most work. Taking a longer step shifts the focus to the glutes, hamstrings, and adductors, engaging the posterior chain. A shorter step places more demand on the quadriceps, increasing knee loading while reducing overall lower body muscle activation.

=== FILE: Side Crow (Parsva Bakāsana)： Balance, Strength & Precision [QGIVlX6eKcY].txt ===

 side-chrome, parsva-bhakasana. An advanced variation of crow pose, side-chrome combines arm balance with a spinal twist. It builds core and shoulder strength, focus, and stability. Precision is key as small shifts in wrists, spine, or shoulder alignment affect balance. Common challenges include lifting into and holding the pose. In the common problems, we address each with solutions to make the asana more accessible. To practice, start in vajrasana, lift the hips, raise the knees, and place the toes on the mat. Twist right, bringing the left elbow across the outside of the right knee, and plant the palms on the floor. Shift weight forward, rest the knees on the arms, and lift both feet. Keep the gaze slightly forward and down, breathe steadily, and engage the core. The lumbar and thoracic spinal vertebrae are in rotation. The cervical vertebrae are in extension. On the right side, the external oblique and the quadratus lumborum are stretched and assist in rotation of the body. The multifidus is also stretched. The right shoulder joint is inflection. The anterior deltoid contracts and stabilizes the shoulder, and the pectoralis major also assists in stabilization. The right elbow joint is inflection. The triceps brachii contract to stabilize the arms. Both wrist joints are in palmar flexion, while the wrist flexor muscles contract. The scapula is in the lower back. The scapula is in protraction. The serratus anterior helps to stabilize the body. Common problem, thoracic stiffness and limited spinal rotation can prevent safe practice of this pose. Side crow requires a solid foundation of spinal mobility and range of motion, and attempting it without this may cause strain or injury. An initial check is recommended. Sit on a block, bring the opposite hand across the thigh, and join the palms together. If holding this position is difficult, spinal rotation should be improved with preparatory exercises. Common problem, limited wrist extension causes pain and overload on the wrist bones. About 90 degrees of wrist extension is needed for safe weight bearing. Without it, forcing the position can strain or injure the base of the palms. Before practicing, bring the forearms and hands together, open the elbows and lower the hands. If the palms separate before 90 degree, mobility is limited. If they stay together, the range is sufficient. To build safe foundations, use preparatory exercises such as dorsiflexion stretches, wrist flexor stretches, finger palm flexion extension, and wrist flexion on blocks. Always work with control and proper technique. Common problem, a weak core, shoulder girdle, or arms can make side crow hard to lift into and sustain. Solution one, elevate the pelvis on a stool or blocks to make shifting into the pose easier. Place hands on the mat and transfer weight forward while supported. Solution two, from the setup, lift one leg while keeping the other foot grounded. Solution three, start with both feet on a block to raise the pelvis, easing the twist and weight transfer. Begin by lifting just one leg. Common problem, stiffness in the lower back can prevent reaching the full pose. Solution, elevate the pelvis, sit on a block or stool to raise the hips. The higher the pelvis, the easier it becomes to enter the pose. From here, place the hands on the mat and gradually shift weight toward the arms while the pelvis remains supported. This elevation reduces strain and makes the assina more accessible. Common problem, lack of confidence and fear of falling forward. Solution, place a cushion or bolster in front of the body. This creates a safe landing surface in case of a fall and reduces the fear of injury. With this support, practitioners can attempt the pose more confidently, gradually building trust in their balance and strength.

=== FILE: Side-Lying Lateral Raise： Target the Delts in a Lengthened Position [5rMrHx2gLLw].en.txt ===

Sidelineing lateral raise incline bench dumbbell. The side lying lateral raise on an incline bench places the deltoid in an elongated position at the start of the movement. Lie on your side on an incline bench, ensuring your body is fully supported. Hold a dumbbell in your top hand with a neutral grip, positioning it in front of your body at the midline to fully stretch the deltoid. Raise your arm laterally in a controlled motion until it reaches just above shoulder height. Slowly lower the dumbbell back to the starting position, maintaining constant tension in the shoulder. Repeat for the desired number of repetitions. Then switch sides. The middle deltoid abducts the shoulder with the assistance of the anterior and posterior deltoid. The supraspanatus plays a crucial role in initiating shoulder abduction. The upper and lower trapezius and the seratus anterior rotate the scapula upward. Lateral raise versus sidelineing. Lateral raise. In a standard lateral raise, the deltoid experiences peak resistance when the arm is parallel to the ground at 90° of shoulder abduction. In a sidelineing lateral raise, maximal resistance occurs much earlier at approximately 45 den of shoulder abduction. This places greater tension on the deltoid in a more stretched position than the standard lateral raise, making it particularly effective for strengthening the muscle in a more lengthened position.

=== FILE: Smith Machine Bent-Over Row： Strengthen Your Back with Control [jOr03vOvMH0].en.txt ===

Bent over row Smith machine. Bent over row strengthens the upper back muscles while stabilizing the movement by engaging the lower back, core, and the entire posterior chain. Stand with your feet shoulderwidth apart and grip the bar with a slightly wider than shoulder width overhand grip. Hinge forward at the hips with a neutral spine and a slight bend at the knees. Initiate the movement by driving your elbows behind your body while retracting the shoulder blades. Pull the bar toward your belly button. Then slowly and with control lower it back to the starting position. Repeat for the desired number of repetitions. The latisimus dorsy extends the shoulders with the assistance of the posterior deltoid. The romboids and middle trapezius retract the scapula. The posterior chain muscles stabilize the body and maintain the bent over position enabling efficient force transfer throughout the movement. Common mistake rounded back during the lift. This exercise is intended to strengthen the latisimus dorsy and the posterior chain. Difficulty performing the lift with a neutral spine may result from tight hamstrings or lack of awareness. To correct this, incorporate hamstring stretches to improve flexibility and focus on engaging the back muscles to maintain a neutral spine throughout the movement.

=== FILE: Spinning Bike： Muscle Engagement & Movement Phases [bvfjVo8wbeg].txt ===

 spinning bike. The spinning bike is a low-impact, high-intensity cardio exercise that improves endurance and allows precise control over workout intensity through resistance adjustments. The pedal stroke has four phases, each with a distinct role in specific muscle actions. In the power phase, the glutus maximus extends the hip, and the quadriceps extend the knee, driving the pedal downward. A slight heel drop engages the hamstrings early to assist with hip extension. In the pullback phase, the hamstrings flex the knee while the calves assist with ankle planter flexion, keeping the movement smooth and continuous. During the upstroke, the hip flexors lift the thigh, and the hamstrings continue to assist with knee flexion. This prevents the leg from becoming passive and reduces drag on the opposite side. The setup phase prepares the leg for the next push. The knee begins to move forward, and the ankle shifts toward dorsiflexion, positioning the body to reengage the glutes and quads as the cycle begins again.
