=== FILE: Squat Jump (Barbell)： Build Explosive Power [hO4lUEXfriY].en.txt ===

Squat jump barbell. The jumping squat with a barbell is a combination of a squat with a jump that improves explosive power. Begin by placing a barbell on your shoulders and then stand with your feet shoulderwidth apart, toes slightly turned out. Lower your body into a squat position while keeping your spine neutral. From the bottom of the squat, generate explosive power to jump off the ground, extending your hips, knees, and ankles. When you land, immediately go back into a squat position and repeat the exercise for the desired number of repetitions. The target muscles are the quadriceps femmeris, glutius maximus, and gastro nemius. The quadriceps femorous muscle extends the knee. The glutius maximus extends the thigh at the hip joint. The hamstrings assist in hip extension. The gastromus and solius plantar flex the foot. Additionally, the core muscles play a crucial role in stabilizing the body during these movements, ensuring efficient force transfer between the upper and lower body. The gas rock nemist has the ability to perform explosive movements in the ankle. It is one of the target muscles in this exercise. In this variation of the squat jump with a barbell, each jump is followed by a brief pause rather than performing continuous jumps. The short pause allows for partial energy recovery, which helps improve maximal explosive power, enabling each jump to be executed with greater force. In contrast, performing jumps consecutively emphasizes endurance of explosive power rather than peak explosive power.

=== FILE: Straight Arm Frog Stand： Strength and Balance [Uf7hb8utUm4].en.txt ===

Straight arm frog stand. The straight arm frog stand exercise is a preparatory exercise for the PLCH. It's designed to develop strength with a particular focus on balance skills. Place your hands on the floor directly under the shoulders and spread your fingers wide. Bring your knees on the outside of your elbows. Look down and press your fingers against the floor. Start leaning forward until you can disconnect the feet off the ground. The target muscles are the wrist flexors, elbow flexors, and anterior deltoid. The wrist flexors maintain balance. The elbow flexors stabilize the elbow against the knees who push the elbows forward. The anterior deltoid flexes the shoulder joint and prevents the body from falling forward with the pectoralis major's assistance. The seratus anterior protects the scapula. The lower trapezius pulls the scapula downward and tilts it posteriorly. Common problem pain in the wrist joint due to high load in a problematic angle for the joint. Solution: Place the hand on a towel or a slightly elevated surface to reduce the joint angle. Another option is to rotate the hands sideways. Common problem sensitivity and pain caused by overload on the wrist bones due to limitations in range of wrist extension movement. A 90° extension angle in the wrist is needed when bearing vertical weight on the hands. If exercisers do not have sufficient range of motion, any attempt to force these positions may result in injury. Hence before performing the position it is important to ensure the practitioner is able to reach this 90° angle in terms of movement potential. There is one simple practical assessment we can use in order to evaluate the specific movement range. One proposed test of passive extension wrist range of motion. A bring forearms and hands together in a prayer position in front of the body. B. Open the elbows sideways and lower the hands down in front of your body until the palms of the hands begin to move away from each other. If the result is a 90° angle between the hands and the forearms without the palms separated, the passive range of motion is sufficient. However, if the palms of the hands start to separate from each other before reaching a 90° angle, the passive range of the wrist extension motion is limited for performing this pose and may injure the base of the palms due to overload pressure. In this condition, it would be essential to use adaptations and practice dedicated preparatory exercises that will prepare the movement foundations for bearing weight on the wrists. Common problem weakness in the shoulder girdle and the elbow extensors creating difficulty in straightening the elbows and bearing the body weight on the hands. Solution one, place the head on a padded surface on the floor between the hands. The head is supported and stabilizes the position. This also provides a feeling of security. It is important to adjust the exact height of the support to the head placement. If the height is too low, the head will not get the needed support and if the surface is too high, the neck will be compressed. Two, elevate the feet on a block. Elevating the feet helps to reach the position and place the legs on the arms. Common problem, rigidity in the lower back and hip joints in sufficient range to reach the position. Solution: Preparatory exercises to soften the lumbar vertebrae area and stretch the lumbar extensors, focusing on elongating the lower back muscles.

=== FILE: The Guide to Types of Grips in Strength Training [tU3q4pNaQEU].en.txt ===

The guide to types of grips in strength training. Let's explore different types of grips and their benefits. First, we have the overhand grip, also known as the standard or pronated grip, which relies on finger and thumb positioning to determine strength. Aligning the thumb with the other fingers, open grip reduces grip strength. While wrapping the thumb over the fingertips, closed grip enhances force. The closed grip is commonly utilized in both pulling and pushing exercises. The open grip variation is commonly used in pulling exercises. In pushing exercises such as bench press, the nickname suicide grip was earned due to safety concerns. Next is the underhand grip or supenated grip. This grip involves greater biceps engagement but requires a full range of wrist supnation which may not be suitable for every exercise. Typical exercises for this grip include barbell curls and chin-ups. The mixed grip, also known as the alternate grip, combines one hand with an overhand grip causing shoulder internal rotation and the other with an underhand grip causing shoulder external rotation. This grip is ideal for lifting heavier weights as it prevents the bar from rolling. It's particularly beneficial for heavy deadlifts and powerlifting because it reduces the strain on the brachioraiialis and flexor cararpiolaris muscles making it easier to maintain during intense lifts. The hook grip involves wrapping your fingers around the bar and hooking your thumb under your fingers. While it provides a secure grip for heavy lifts, it can be uncomfortable or painful for the thumb. The hook grip is particularly common in Olympic lifts such as snatches, clean, and jerks. Compared to the mixed grip, the hook grip provides stability during explosive movements without limiting the range of motion. Additionally, it promotes a more balanced shoulder position as both hands are internally rotated. The false grip, also known as the thumbless grip, is a technique where the wrist rather than just the fingers engages with the bar or gymnastic rings. Here, the bar is positioned close to the wrist crease, which differs from a standard grip that aligns with the palm. This strategic grip is crucial for facilitating smoother transitions between complex movements. It is particularly valuable in exercises like the muscle up, which integrates a pull-up with a dip. By employing the false grip, athletes can fluidly progress from the pulling phase directly into the dip phase without the need to readjust their grip. In summary, there are several distinct grips, each designed for specific purposes. These include the overhand grip, underhand grip, mix grip, hook grip, and false grip. Each has different benefits and should be used according to your training goals.

=== FILE: The Hack Squat Guide： Form, Muscles, and Mistakes to Avoid [36_EC-MgVz8].txt ===

 hack squat machine. The hack squat is a compound lower-body exercise that allows for heavy quadriceps loading in a stable, controlled environment. It minimizes lower back stress and offers a more beginner-friendly alternative to free weight squats. Stand on the platform with your back against the pad and shoulders under the support pads, and place your feet shoulder-width apart. Unlock the safety handles and slowly lower the sled by bending at your hips and knees, keeping your back flat against the pad. Descend until your thighs are parallel to the platform or slightly below, then push through your heels to return to the starting position. Repeat for the desired number of repetitions. The glutus maximus extends the hip with the assistance of the hamstrings, increasing their contribution as hip flexion becomes deeper. Due to the machine's back support, activation of the core and spinal stabilizers is minimal. The calves contribute to ankle stability throughout the entire movement. Common mistake, lifting the heels off the platform at the bottom of the squat, shifting the pressure toward the toes. To avoid this mistake, keep your entire foot flat on the platform, especially the heels. Adjust your foot position if needed and descend only as far as your ankle mobility allows while maintaining full foot contact. Common mistake, placing the feet too high on the platform. This often leads to compensating with lumbar spine flexion, causing the pelvis to lift off the machine toward the end of the range of motion instead of achieving proper hip flexion. As a result, glute engagement is reduced and unnecessary strain is placed on the lower back. To correct this, position your feet lower on the platform, allowing your knees to move forward as you descend. Common mistake, dropping too quickly. Dropping too quickly during the eccentric phase reduces the load and relies on the stretch reflex during the concentric phase. This is less effective for exercises focused on hypertrophy as it shifts the focus away from controlled muscle engagement and strength development in favor of explosive power.

=== FILE: The Sciatic Nerve Explained： Anatomy, Pathway & Branches [Qt6ZkrWAnnM].en.txt ===

The sciatic nerve. The sciatic nerve is the longest and largest nerve in the human body. It arises from the lumbossacral plexus and has root contributions from L4 to S3. It is composed of two divisions. The tibial division which runs medially and the common fibular or paranal division which runs laterally. Both are bundled together in a common sheath. It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic fammen passing beneath the puraphiforis muscle and descends through the glutial region into the posterior thigh. Here it lies deep to the glutius maximus and superficial to the adductor magnus running deep to the long head of the biceps feorous as it descends through the posterior thigh. Along its course, the tibial division of the sciatic nerve gives motor branches to the long head of the biceps for feorous semiendinosis, semimebrosinosis and the isial part of the adductor magnus. The common fibular division provides motor intervation to the short head of the biceps for at the top of the poplil fossa. The two bundled divisions of the sciatic nerve separate into their terminal branches. The tibial nerve and the common fibular nerve also known as the paranal nerve. The tibial nerve continues vertically through the midline of the popl fossa. Then descends in the posterior compartment of the leg. It gives muscular branches to the gastromus, solius, plantaris, paplatus, tibialis posterior, flexor digtorum longus and flexor hallucus longus. At the level of the medial malololis, it passes posterior to it and beneath the flexor retinacculum where it divides into the medial and lateral plantar nerves. These branches innervate the intrinsic muscles of the foot and provide sensory supply to the sole and toes. Additional nerves emerging from this region include the medial and inferior calccanial nerves. The medial calccanial nerve arises proximal to the flexor retinacculum providing cutaneous intervation to the posterior medial aspect of the heel. The inferior calccanial nerve also known as baxter's nerve is a branch of the lateral plantar nerve. It provides motor intervation to the abductor digiti minimi muscle and sensory supply to the calccanial perryostium and adjacent skin of the heel. Shifting focus back to the popl fossa. The second branch diverging from the sciatic nerve is the common fibular nerve. It travels laterally along the tendon of the biceps for feorous then curves around the neck of the fibula. From there it splits into the superficial and deep fibular nerves. The superficial fibular nerve continues through the lateral compartment supplying the fibularis longus and brevis and then divides into two sensory branches. The medial dorsal cutaneous nerve and the intermediate dorsal cutaneous nerve which provide cutaneous sensation to the lower lateral leg and the dorsome of the foot. The deep fibular nerve enters the anterior compartment supplying the tibialis anterior extensor digtorum longus extensor hallucus longus fibularis terteius extensor digtorum brevvis and extensor hallucus brevis and finally gives off its cutaneous branch in the web space between the first two toes. Additionally, another sensory pathway branches from the sciatic nerve, the suroral nerve. It is formed by the union of the medial suralcutaneous nerve from the tibial nerve and the suroral communicating branch from the common fibular nerve. Together they supply sensation to the lower posterior lateral leg and the lateral side of the foot. In summary, the sciatic nerve supplies motor intervation to the posterior thigh and through its tibial and fibular branches to all muscles of the leg and foot. It provides sensory intervation to the posterior lateral leg, dorsome and sole of the foot and the area between the first and second toes.

=== FILE: Think Light Weights Are Easy？ Think Again! [raiQkSDeZbM].en.txt ===

Think lightweights are easy? Think again. Research shows that high rep lightweight sets cause more fatigue even in muscles you're not training and take longer to recover from than heavy lifting. Why? Total work. For example, if your max is 100 kg, a heavy set of three reps at 90 kg equals 270 kg of total work. But a light set of 30 reps at 30 kg equals a massive 900 kg of work. That's over three times as much, meaning longer recovery. So whether you're lifting heavy or light, remember both approaches have their place. Light weights are great for endurance, while heavy weights are better for strength.

=== FILE: Try the 3 to 7 Training Method! 💪 [Ra_BfFye7sM].en.txt ===

the 3 to7 Training Method the 3 to7 protocol is a strength training method designed to develop maximal strength and hypertrophy this method is characterized by 3 to seven repetitions using a load of approximately 70% of your one repetition maximum with a brief rest interval of 15 seconds between sets this method is unique in that the prescribed training volume is collapsed into a very short period let's take the standing biceps curl exercise for examp example choose a dumbbell suitable for performing 10 repetitions start by performing only three repetitions rest for only 15 seconds and then perform four repetitions rest for 15 seconds perform five repetitions and so on up to seven repetitions the final block of repetitions six and seven should lead you to muscle failure performing one cycle of 3 to 7 can replace three standard sets of 3 * 8 or 3 * 10 try the method and feel how your muscles react to it always ensure you are training safely especially when using heavy weights and working towards muscle failure

=== FILE: Type of muscle contraction [bAQABuMxHec].en.txt ===

Types of Muscle Contraction There are three general types of muscle contraction that help us produce different movements: Isotonic muscle contractions, Isometric muscle contractions, And isokinetic muscle contractions. Let's start with isotonic muscle contraction. Isotonic muscle contraction is the best-known type of contraction. It is characterized by a change in muscle length during contraction without a change in load or resistance. There are two types of isotonic muscle contractions: concentric contraction and eccentric contraction. Concentric contraction: This is characterized by muscle shortening under load. For example, when you perform a bicep curl, during the flexion phase, your biceps contract and shorten under the load. Eccentric contraction: This is the opposite of isotonic muscle contraction, meaning the muscle is lengthened under load. In the same example, eccentric contraction will occur when you lower extend your elbow during the bicep curl, and the biceps will lengthen under load. We can see another example of these two contraction types in the quadriceps muscle when performing the box step-up. When stepping up on the box, the quadriceps shorten under load, meaning concentric contraction. When stepping back to the starting position, The quadriceps lengthen under load eccentric contraction, which also helps to restrain the body weight and prevent falling. The second type of muscle contraction is isometric muscle contraction. Unlike isotonic muscle contraction, in isometric muscle contraction, there is no change in muscle length during contraction. For example, in the plank exercise, the rectus abdominals stabilizes the trunk without a change in its length. If we look at the bicep curl again, Isometric contraction means you would stop during the movement and statically hold the weight without flexing or extending your elbow. And lastly, we have isokinetic muscle contraction. Isokinetic contractions are like isotonic in that the muscle changes in length during the contraction. The difference between isotonic contractions and these contractions is that the isokinetic contractions produce movements of a constant speed. Examples of using isokinetic contractions in daily activity and sports are rare. However, the best example of this type of contraction is the breaststroke when swimming, Where the water provides a constant, even resistance to the adduction movement. To produce this type of contraction, you would need to be using an isokinetic dynamometer system. In summary, we talked about three types of muscle contraction: isotonic, isometric, and isokinetic. Isotonic contraction includes concentric contraction where the muscle is shortened under load, And eccentric contraction where the muscle is lengthened under load. Isometric contraction when the muscle doesn't change in length under load. And isokinetic contraction when the muscle changes in length during contraction and produces movements of a constant speed.

=== FILE: Types of Synovial Joints [dJZz7hBoALs].en.txt ===

types of synovial joints synovial joints are the freely mobile joints in which the articulating surfaces have no direct contact with each other ball and socket where the ball shaped surface of one rounded bone fits into the cup-like depression of another bone it permits free movement in numerous axes for example the hip joint condyloid contains a convex surface which articulates with a concave elliptical cavity they are also known as ellipsoid joints for example metacarpal phalangeal joint wrist joint gliding plane the articular surfaces are relatively flat allowing the bones to glide over one another for example acromial clavicular joint subtiller joint hinge permits movement in one place usually flexion and extension for example elbow joint knee joint pivot allows for rotation only it is formed by a central bony pivot for example proximal and distal radial ulnar joints saddle named due to its resemblance to a saddle on a horse's back it is characterized by opposing articular surfaces with a reciprocal concave convex shape for example carpal metacarpal joints download our app for fast and easy learning becoming a real professional is a couple clicks away

=== FILE: Ulnar Nerve： Anatomy & Function [WCWBb6zupco].txt ===

 the ulnar nerve. The ulnar nerve originates from the medial cord of the brachial plexus, with root contributions from C8 and T1. From the medial cord of the brachial plexus, it descends along the medial side of the upper arm toward the elbow. In the arm it courses posterior to the medial epicondyle within the cubital tunnel and enters the forearm between the two heads of the flexor carpi ulnaris. In the forearm, it innervates the flexor carpi ulnaris and the medial half of the flexor digitorum profundus, which controls flexion of the fourth and fifth digits. From there, it travels toward the wrist, passing superficial to the flexor retinaculum within guillon's canal. At the wrist, the ulnar nerve divides into superficial and deep branches. The deep branch innervates the hypothenar muscles, oponens digiti minimi, flexor digiti minimi brevis, and abductor digiti minimi. From the hypothenar muscles, the deep branch courses laterally across the palm. It innervates all of the dorsal and palmar interossei. The third and fourth lumbricles, the deep head of the flexor plexus brevis, and the adductor plexus. The superficial branch supplies the palmaris brevis and provides sensory innervation to the palmar surfaces of the medial one and a half digits, as well as the adjacent palmar skin at their bases. In addition, the palmar cutaneous branch passes superficial to the flexor retinaculum to supply the skin over the hypothenar eminence. The dorsal cutaneous branch innervates the skin on the dorsum of the medial hand, and the dorsal aspects of the medial one and a half fingers. In summary, the ulnar nerve arises from the medial cord of the brachial plexus, with root contributions from C8 and T1. It provides motor innervation to the flexor carpi ulnaris, the medial half of the flexor digitorum profundus, and most intrinsic muscles of the hand, as well as sensory supply to the palmar and dorsal aspects of the medial one and a half digits with the adjacent palm.

=== FILE: Understanding Axes of Movement⧸Rotation [bXkrFmbvnKU].en.txt ===

axis of rotation bones rotate around a joint in a plane that is perpendicular to an axis of rotation axis of rotation is an imaginary line that projects through the pivot or rotation point in a joint ap axis anterior posterior an imaginary line running from anterior to posterior and perpendicular to the frontal plane mgl axis medial lateral or transverse an imaginary line running from left to right and perpendicular to sagittal plane longitudinal axis an imaginary line running from top to bottom and perpendicular to transverse plane sagittal plane rotations occur about a medial lateral ml axis frontal plane rotations occur about an anterior posterior ap axis transverse plane rotations occur about a longitudinal axis the shoulder for example allows movement in all three planes and therefore has three axes of rotation in the elbow example the forearm moves in the sagittal plane of motion as the elbow joint flexes and extends because flexion and extension occur in an anterior and posterior direction respectively the axis of rotation is perpendicular to it and projects through the joint in a medial lateral or side to side direction hence for the elbow movement occurs in the sagittal has flexion and extension around a medial lateral axis of rotation deepen your understanding of the human body check out our apps sign up for free you

=== FILE: Understanding Muscle Contraction： How Nerves Control Your Movements [gpfhkvae2FU].en.txt ===

motor unit the human body contains many skeletal muscles muscles that are in charge of moving our body any voluntary movement starts with a command from the brain this command goes down through the spinal cord exits through a motor neuron and goes to the muscle in this video we will learn about the somatic motor unit and how the muscles and nerves work together to perform different tasks the central nervous system controls every skeletal muscle through an alpha motor neuron the Alpha motor neuron cell body is located in the spinal cord at the vental horn the axon exits through the vental roots and inserts into muscle fiber a motor neuron axon branches many times after entering the target muscle and each branch makes its way to different muscle fibers fibers and forms a small cluster of terminal branches when a motor neuron fires all the muscle fibers in the motor unit contract at the same time the size of a motor unit varies from just a few fibers in the eye muscles precise movements to over a thousand threads in the large leg muscles powerful movements the all or nun law this principle states that if a single motor unit receives a stimulus of sufficient intensity to bring forth a response all the muscle fibers within that unit will contract at the same time and to the maximum extent if the intensity or duration of the stimulus is increased the height of the impulse will remain the same the nerve fiber either gives a maximal response or none at all however the muscle can regulate its strength by activating only the required number of motor units needed for a specific action for example if a train performs squats without additional weight a small number of motor units will be recruited together but when a trainee does the squat with heavy weights more motor units will be required to complete the exercise in summary when you want to activate a muscle for a specific task the brain sends a signal through the spinal cord the signal exits the spinal cord through the alha a motor neuron and travels along the axon until it reaches the target muscle then the axon branches and transmits the signal to the muscle fiber through the neuromuscular Junction

=== FILE: Understanding Type 1 Fibers #muscleandmotion #anatomy [MZWyfi9gs84].en.txt ===

type 1 fibers are known as slow oxidative or twitch fibers these fibers contract relatively slowly and have a high number of mitochondria within each cell that uses oxygen to produce energy known as adenosine triphosphate ATP they produce low power contractions over long periods of time and are slow to fatigue because slow twitch fibers function for long periods without fatiguing they are used during low intensity activities such as maintaining posture and stabilizing bones

slippery substance that allows the surfaces to slide against one another without damage to either surface the function of articular cartilage is to absorb shock and provide an extremely smooth surface to facilitate motion the ligaments the knee consists of many ligaments ligaments are tough bands of tissue that connect the ends of bones together the ligaments protect the knee and provide stability let's take a look at the four most important ligaments first we have the medial collateral ligament or MCL and the lateral collateral ligament or LCL are found on either side of the knee joint the MCL and LCL prevent the knee from moving too far in the side to side Direction inside the knee joint there are two other important ligaments that stretch between the femur and the tibia these are the anterior cruciate ligament ACL at the front and the posterior cruciate ligament PCL at the back the ACO keeps the tibia from sliding too far forward in relation to the femur and the PCO keeps the tibia from sliding too far backward in relation to the femur together the ACL and PCO control the back and forth motion of the knee meniscus there are two meniski in the space between the femoral and tibial condyles the medial meniscus and the lateral the meniscus of the knee are important for two main reasons one they work like a gasket to spread the force from the weight of the body over a larger area and protect the knee joint cartilage two they help stabilize the knee by acting like a wedge the meniscus are thicker around the outside and this thickness helps keep the round Femur from rolling on the flat tibia movement the ligaments and meniscus provide static stability and the muscles and tendons Dynamic stability the main movement of the knee is flexion extension for that matter the knee acts as a hinge joint whereby the articular surfaces of the femur roll and glide over the tibial surface during flexion and extension the tibia and patella act as one structure in relation to the femur the knee joint also includes some rotational movement between the femur and the tibia this rotational movement is part of the screw home mechanism which is a key element of knee stability it occurs at the end of knee extension between full extension zero degrees and 20 degrees of knee flexion muscles and tendons there are many muscles surrounding the knee joint those muscles move the knee joint back and forth when the muscles contract the tendons are pulled and the bone is moved first let's talk about the extensor muscles of the knee joint the primary extensor muscles of the knee joint are the quadriceps femoris assisted by the tensor fasciae latte the quadriceps femoris is comprised of four heads three heads originate from the femur and the fourth head the rectus femoris originates the tibial tuberosity by the patella ligament the tensor fasciae latte is attached to the iliotibial band the iliotibial band extends further inferiorly and inserts into the lateral condyle of the the knee joint function and movement we divided the different structures into a few main categories bones joints ligaments meniscus and lastly muscles and tendons

=== FILE: Understanding the Skeletal System： Types and Functions of Bones [X3IOfTfh1Xs].en.txt ===

The Skeletal System Bone is a calcified, living, connective tissue that forms most of the skeletal system in the human body. There are, on average, 206 bones. The bones have many functions. Such as supporting the structures of the body, Protecting vital organs, Storage for minerals and contain blood producing cells, Levers for the muscles to act on to produce movement. The bones in the body are generally categorized into five types, and they are usually classified by their shape: Long Short Flat Irregular And the sesamoid bones. We will now take a closer look at each bone type. Let's start with the long bones. These bones are longer than they are wide, they consist of a long shaft with two bulky ends or extremities, much like the classic dog bone. They are primarily compact bones, but may have a large amount of spongy bone at the ends or extremities. Long bones include: thigh, leg, arm, and forearm bones. Next we have the short bones. Short bones are called that because they are about as wide as they are long. They consist primarily of spongy bone tissue, which is covered by a thin layer of compact bone. Short bones include the carpal bones of the hands that allow movement of the wrist. And the tarsal bones of the feet that enable ankle movement. Flat bones. Flat bones are thin, flattened and usually slightly curved. Flat bones include your ribs, sternum, scapula and most of the skull bones. Now let's look at the irregular bones. As their name implies, these bones have irregular shapes and structures and cannot be defined by any specific character. Examples of irregular bones include the vertebrae, sacrum, coccyx, and some of the skull bones. Finally we have the sesamoid bones. A sesamoid bone is a small bone commonly found embedded within a muscle or tendon near joint surfaces. It functions as a pulley to alleviate stress on that muscle or tendon. Unlike other bones, which connect via joints, sesamoid bones connect to muscles via tendons. Sesamoid bones are commonly located in the foot, hand and wrist, With the patella being the largest and most well known sesamoid bone. To summarize, we've talked about the five different bone types: The long bones such as the thigh, leg, arm and forearm bones. The short bones, such as the carpal bones and the tarsal bones. The flat bones such as the sternum, ribs and scapular bones. The irregular bones, such as the vertebrae and the sacrum, bones. And the sesamoid bones such as the patella bone.

=== FILE: Unlock Flexibility and Stability with Deer Pose [DG9XaIbe1t8].en.txt ===

Deer pose migasana is a seated posture that provides gentle twist and hip stretch. The deer pose can be performed while sitting in a wide number of variations both actively and for restorative purposes to help to release many tension points in the hip, pelvic, and back joints. Start by sitting in the bound angle pose. Inhale and with an exhalation, move one leg backward while keeping the knee bent. Keep the spine elongated with a slight rotation or flexed and relaxed facing forward. Breathe in and out slowly while relaxing into the pose. The feet gradually move away from the hip joints. The back foot is in dorsal flexion. The front foot is in inversion. The top of the foot is laid on the floor. The hands are used for support. One hand is placed on the floor and the other supports the front knee. In the full position, the feet move away in opposite directions from one another. One moving away from the front hip joint and the other from the rear hip joint. The sitting position in the deer pose is marked by enhanced rotation of the hip joints. The thigh of the front leg is in external rotation with flexion and the thigh of the rear leg is in internal rotation with abduction. In the thigh of the front leg, the hip internal rotator muscles are stretched. Among them, the buttock muscles with an emphasis on the glutius medius and maximus. In addition, the purapiforis is stretched by the combination of flexion and external hip rotation. In the back leg, the hip adductors and hip external rotators are stretched. Mainly the quadratus femmeris is stretched by internal hip rotation with abduction. Here's one common problem when performing this pose. Increased rotation in the front knee joint resulting from limited external rotation in the hip joint. The reason for that could be shortened internal rotators or limited mobility in the ligaments around the hip joint with an emphasis on the lateral portion. Practitioners with a suitable natural structure and high flexibility in the hip joints can perform the full pose relatively easily as their thighs can rotate outward fully. For many practitioners, the hip joint structure disables the femur's range of rotation, and a chain reaction of excessively strong rotation is created in the knee. If the student or teacher forcefully pushes the body down, the tibia and femur will serve as two levers creating large rotational force on the knee. Therefore, there is an actual risk of knee injury when a combination of three components is created at the same time. A flexion, B rotation, C load. This combination is exactly the danger of the pigeon pose if it is performed incorrectly. The practitioner should feel the stretch only in the hip joint. If the stretch moves to the knee, you should exit the pose immediately but carefully. Pressing the legs while applying force into the position is one of the most dangerous things in the pigeon pose and can lead to severe injury in the supporting ligaments of the knee joints. Never force or pressure a student to perform or stay in this position if they feel tenderness or pain in the knee joint. In any case of knee pain, do not perform this pose at all. A possible solution for this problem. Each practitioner has a different range of motion in the hip joint and the pose needs to be adapted personally according to this range. There are a few possible solutions in case of knee pain in the pigeon pose. A reduce the angle between the knee and thigh to avoid rotation. See image below. As the angle increases, so does rotation in the knee. B. Choose an alternative exercise to improve flexibility and mobility of the hip joint. The following is a recommended variation using the wall to improve joint. C. In any case of limited lateral rotation in the hip or sensitivity in the knee, use adaptations to maintain a balanced position of the pelvis. For example, sitting on a block or a bolster to create elevation on one side of the pelvis. D. When the pose is performed fully, the front ankle should be in dorsif flexion. However, if the thigh rotation is limited and there is still pressure on the knee, it is essential to keep the knee joint in the sagittal plane and the ankle in planter flexion point. In this position, even though the knee is bent, the dorsal flexion in the ankle creates rotation and a risk to the knee. Although the knee is in flexion in this position, the ankle is in dorsif flexion causing rotation and endangering the knee. In this position, it is best to keep the ankle in plantar flexion. Plantar flexion in the ankle is more suitable as it decreases rotation in the knee joint and reduces the risk of injury. Hey. Common problem excessive muscle tension. Joint imposed movement limitation, tension throughout the spine. Solution: Additional variations for performing the position with an emphasis on restorative variations. In other words, without muscle effort to release tension points in the spine and hip joints.

=== FILE: Why can a smaller athlete out-jump a massive powerlifter？ [OPoCcaB6ojU].en.txt ===

Why can a smaller athlete out jump a massive powerlifter? Because strength is not power. Strength is about max force. Power is force times velocity. === FILE: World’s Greatest Stretch： Full-Body Mobility in Motion [nnbYao-5bSI].txt ===

 The next exercise in our program is called the world's greatest stretch, but this is not your basic stretch. It's a dynamic movement that hits multiple body parts and planes of motion. It targets your hip flexors, hamstrings, glutes, and upper back mobility all in one. Start in a kneeling position, place one hand on the floor opposite your front leg, and straighten your back leg. Next, squeeze your glute and simultaneously push your knee out to the side while keeping your foot firmly planted on the ground. This should bring out a light stretch to your inner groin muscles. Then, drop your elbow as far towards the ground as possible. While I can drop really far, just go as deep for now as you can. Then, rotate in the opposite direction as far as possible while lifting your arm to the ceiling. This part of the movement targets your mid-back or thoracic spine, and if you keep your back leg straight and squeeze that glute as well, you may even feel your hip flexor stretch if they're really tight. Then, switch sides and perform the same motion with your other leg in front as well.

=== FILE: 🏋️‍♂️ Back Squat vs. Leg Extension： Which Exercise Delivers More Muscle and Strength Gains？ [UEEIYEjwKa0].en.txt ===

Back squat versus leg extension. Which builds more muscle and strength? A recent study found that after eight weeks of training, the leg extension led to greater growth in the rectus feorous while the back squat increased the vastis lateralis especially in its lower region. Why does this happen? The rectus femmeris crosses both the hip and the knee. During a squat, the rectus feorous lengthens at one joint while shortening at the other, which limits its ability to generate tension. In the leg extension, the hip remains stable. So, the muscle focuses entirely on extending the knee, leading to more growth. When it comes to strength, back squat training significantly improves squat performance. But both groups showed similar increases in leg extension strength.

=== FILE: 🔑 The Secret to Smooth Shoulder Flexion？ Scapular Control 🔑 [Hn1eopBne-A].en.txt ===

Think shoulder flexion is all about the front deltoid? Not quite. To lift your arm properly, the scapula must remain stable and move in sync. Lack of stability causes the scapula to shift forward and tilt. Two key muscles help prevent that. The serratus anterior anchors the scapula to the rib cage and the lower trapezius pulls it downward. These muscles not only move the scapula but also stabilize it dynamically during arm movements. Without proper scapular control, shoulder flexion becomes less efficient. So next time you raise your arm, stabilize the scapula first.

Four, cable kickback. isolates the glutes and burns them out with full control. Want dozens more key glute exercises?

=== FILE: 🔥 Turkish Get-Up Kettlebell Challenge 🔥 [B4jgMyhPNLE].en.txt ===

Ready to challenge [music] your strength, stability, and coordination? Take on the Turkish Get Up Kettlebell Challenge. Start on your back with the kettle bell pressed straight up. Lock the elbow, eyes on the bell. Roll to the elbow, then to the hand. Lift the hips high and smoothly sweep the leg back into [music] a tall half kneeling position. Keep the kettle bell stacked in a straight line from shoulder to hip. Drive through the front [music] heel and stand tall with the kettle bell overhead. Now reverse [music] the entire sequence with the same control. No rushing, no collapsing. Every [music] phase matters. Think you've got it? Share your video. Tag musclein motion and show us your skills.

=== FILE: 🚴‍♂️ The 4 Phases of the Pedal Stroke — Explained in Motion! [tlj7o9rceeM].en.txt ===

Ever wondered what really happens during a pedal stroke? Each phase activates different muscles working in [music] perfect sync? In the power phase, your glutes and quads drive the [music] pedal down. As you pull back, the hamstrings and calves keep the motion smooth. During the upstroke, the hip flexors lift the leg while the hamstrings stay engaged. Finally, the setup phase prepares the body for the next powerful [music] push. Want to dive deeper and become an expert in movement analysis? Explore [music] every phase in 3D with the muscle and motion strength training app and take [music] your understanding of biomechanics to the next level.

=== FILE: 🧠 Mobility Check： Front Rack Challenge 🏋️‍♂️ [KwR411MxEJI].en.txt ===

Mobility check. Front rack challenge. How flexible are your wrists and shoulders? Try holding a proper front rack position. Elbows high at or slightly above bar level. Bar rests on your shoulders, not in your hands. Torso stays upright. Wrists extended but relaxed with all fingers gripping the bar. The challenge. Perform a front squat while maintaining a strong front rack. No discomfort, no compensation. If you're feeling pressure or losing form, it might be time to work on your mobility. Think you got it? Tag Muscle in Motion in your video and get featured.
